⏺ Reverted — worktree is clean again. I now have a fully confirmed root cause.
  Here are your answers.

  1) The last action you staged and saved

  It was the gap annotation (הערת שיבוץ) "123" on the חברתי (social) role — slot
  #2 (social#1).

  Tracing the four saveStagedChanges in the log:

  ┌─────┬──────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  #  │  Save    │                What was staged & saved                 │
  │     │   time   │                                                        │
  ├─────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1   │ 17:18:54 │ Created the חקירה (investigation) assignment           │
  ├─────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2   │ 17:19:10 │ Cleared/deleted that investigation assignment ← last   │
  │     │          │ member change                                          │
  ├─────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3   │ 17:19:35 │ Annotation-only on social#0 (the buggy no-op re-save)  │
  ├─────┼──────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 4   │ 17:19:46 │ Annotation "123" on social#1 ← last staged + saved     │
  └─────┴──────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  It was staged at 17:19:42 and saved at 17:19:46. Everything after that was not
  a staged-save: the 17:19:52 quota decrement is an event edit (goes straight
  through EventBloc), and 17:20:01 was "discard all" (which threw away an
  auto-generated re-key). I verified against production — the event doc still
  holds exactly { "social#1": { note: "123", labelId: null } }.

  2) The "dirty on no-change save" bug — confirmed root cause

  Your instinct is right, and I reproduced it with a test (canonical case
  passes, drifted case fails):

  - ✅ A note stored at its own key (social#0 shown on slot 0) → reopen → save
  unchanged → correctly a no-op.
  - ❌ A note that has drifted (stored under social#1 but displayed on slot 0
  after a quota change) → reopen → save unchanged → wrongly marked dirty.

  The mechanism is in StagedSlotAnnotation.isNoop:

  bool get isNoop => desired == baseline && staleKey == null;

  When a note's stored key ≠ the gap it renders on, staleKey is non-null, so
  isNoop is false even when you changed nothing — by design, to "self-heal" the
  drifted key on Save. That's exactly your case: after your quota 2→1 decrement,
  "123" is stored at social#1 but the only visible gap is slot 0, so opening it
  and hitting שמירה dirties the row purely to re-key it. (It drifted in the
  first place because the decrement's auto re-key got thrown away by your
  "discard all" at 17:20:01.)

  The gap: there's a test for editing a drifted note, but none for reopening it 
  unchanged — so this slipped through.

  Before I implement, two quick decisions:

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · How should a no-change save on a drifted note behave? → True no-op 
     (recommended)
     · Where should I make the fix? (The annotation feature is unmerged on 
     feat/empty-slot-gap-annotations, so branching from origin/main wouldn't 
     have the code.) → Existing feature worktree

⏺ Both decisions noted: true no-op fix, in the existing feature worktree —
  .claude/worktrees/empty-slot-gap-annotations (branch
  feat/empty-slot-gap-annotations). That's the worktree to cd into for testing.

  One important nuance I found while planning: isNoop is used in two places —
  the user-edit path (_onStageSlotAnnotation) and the auto-cleanup
  (_stageSlotAnnotationCleanup), where a pure re-key (staleKey != null, content
  unchanged) must stay non-noop so quota-change drift-healing keeps working. So
  the fix must change only the user path, not isNoop globally. Let me read the
  model test to align naming/coverage:

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Now I'll implement the fix. First, add a content-only no-op getter to the
  model (keeping isNoop intact for the auto-cleanup):

      41 +  /// True when the desired note+label equal the baseline — the
         + USER changed                                                  
      42 +  /// nothing. Unlike [isNoop] this IGNORES [staleKey]: re-keyi
         +ng a drifted                                                   
      43 +  /// stored key is the quota auto-cleanup's job, NOT something
         + a user's                                                      
      44 +  /// unchanged Save should stage. Used by `_onStageSlotAnnotat
         +ion` so that

         +ng never lights                                                
      46 +  /// the row up as dirty just to self-heal the key.

         +] set) is                                                      
      52 +  /// intentionally NOT a noop here, so a quota change still he
         +als the DB key.                                                
      53 +  bool get isNoop => isContentNoop && staleKey == null;

  Summary

  The fix (true no-op, in feat/empty-slot-gap-annotations):

  - staged_slot_annotation.dart — added isContentNoop => desired == baseline
  (staleKey-agnostic), and redefined isNoop => isContentNoop && staleKey == null
  (unchanged behavior).
  - assignment_bloc.dart — the user-edit handler _onStageSlotAnnotation now
  drops the edit when isContentNoop (not isNoop). So reopening a note and
  pressing שמירה without changing anything never marks the row dirty — even when
  the stored key drifted. The auto-cleanup still uses isNoop, so quota-change
  drift-healing is untouched.
  - Added regression tests: canonical no-op, drifted no-op (your exact case),
  and "a real drifted edit still stages + self-heals" so the fix doesn't neuter
  genuine edits.

  Verdict: not a state bug — a misleading log

  The discard-all logic is correct. What happened at 17:20:01–02:

  1. Your quota decrement (social 2→1) left the "123" note out-of-range, so the
  auto-cleanup staged a re-key of social#1 (one entry in _stagedSlotAnnotations,
  zero in _stagedChanges).
  2. The discard dialog's count reads stagedSlotKeys (the union of both maps) →
  correctly showed 1.
  3. DiscardAllStagedChanges cleared both maps (line 1981 clears annotations) —
  so it did discard that 1 annotation.
  4. But the stage:discardAll {had} log only printed _stagedChanges.length → 0,
  making it look like a phantom "dialog said 1, had 0" desync.

  So: correct behavior, wrong log. (This is also the same auto re-key you then
  threw away, which is why social#1 stayed drifted.)

  Fixed stage:discardAll to log both changes and annotations — flutter analyze
  clean. It's a log-string-only change (no behavior), so no new test needed.

⏺ You've found a real one — and a sharp question. Let me investigate both before
  answering. First let me trace issue 1 precisely against the code, and

  Read 1 file, ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I traced both. Here's what's going on.

  Issue 1 — where the "123" came from

  It came from the database. Your event still had social#1 = "123" stored (the
  drifted note from yesterday). Here's the sequence the log shows:

  1. While social quota was 1, social#1 was out of range, so it drifted onto the
  only visible row (display slot 0) and showed "123".
  2. You edited that row "123" → "456" and staged it (18:08:20, stagedCount: 1).
  That staged edit is anchored to display slot 0 and carries staleKey: 
  "social#1" (a snapshot saying "the real note lives at social#1").
  3. You bumped social quota 1 → 2. Now social#1 is in range again → it's
  canonical and reconcile puts it back on slot 1, showing "123". Meanwhile your
  staged "456" is still pinned to slot 0.

  So the one note split into two rows: staged "456" on slot 0, live DB "123" on
  slot 1. Nothing created "123" — the quota bump un-hid the original DB value at
  its natural slot while your edit stayed where you made it.

  There's a worse half you didn't hit yet: if you'd pressed Save, the staged
  entry would write social#0 = "456" and delete staleKey social#1 — so the "123"
  row you're looking at would silently vanish. The staleKey snapshot went stale
  the moment the quota changed.

  Root cause: staged annotation edits are anchored by display slot index + a
  snapshot staleKey, and both become invalid when a quota change renumbers slots
  between staging and saving. It only happens to drifted notes (a canonical
  note's slot doesn't move, so it never splits).

  Straight answer on tests: no, I didn't cover this, and you're right to push.
  My fix + tests covered the no-op case and drift at a fixed quota; the existing
  suite covers quota changes only for auto-generated cleanup entries — not a 
  user edit surviving a quota change. This bug lives exactly in that untested
  gap. To be clear it's pre-existing (this was a real 123→456 edit, so my
  isContentNoop change neither caused nor touched it) — but that's an
  explanation, not an excuse. It should have a test.

  Issue 2 — custom role keys vs the medic#1 format

  Admin-created roles do not use the Hebrew name for the key.
  RoleRepository.createRole generates:

  final key = 'role_${DateTime.now().millisecondsSinceEpoch}';   // e.g. 
  role_1721842143567

  So a custom role's annotation key is role_1721842143567#0,
  role_1721842143567#1, etc. Since the key never contains #, and
  parseSlotAnnotationKey splits on the last #, it round-trips correctly — no 
  parsing bug. (The _ in the key is also safe: the event_role_index display key
  is only ever used as an opaque map key, never parsed back.) One minor
  unrelated robustness note: two roles created in the same millisecond would
  collide on that key — rare, but real.

  Proposed next step

  I'd like to fix Issue 1 properly and add the missing test. Two viable
  approaches:

  - A — suppress the duplicate at display (smaller): when a stored note's key is
  already claimed as a live staged edit's staleKey, don't also render it as its
  own gap. Result: slot 0 = "456", slot 1 = empty; Save stays consistent.
  - B — re-anchor on quota change (more correct, bigger): when a quota change
  makes a staged edit's staleKey canonical again, migrate the edit to follow its
  underlying note (and drop the now-invalid staleKey).

  I lean A — it kills the split and the silent-delete-on-Save, with far less
  blast radius on the reconciliation logic, plus regression tests for the exact
  "staged edit on a drifted note, then change quota" sequence. Want me to
  implement A, or go with B?

✻ Churned for 4m 31s

  What the fix does

  In _effectiveGapAnnotation — the single choke point both slot-build paths use
  — a stored note is now suppressed from rendering as its own gap when a live 
  staged edit already owns it via staleKey:

  if (reconciled != null && _stagedEditClaimsSourceKey(eventId,
  reconciled.sourceKey)) {
    return null;   // the staged edit owns this note; don't render it twice
  }

  So in your exact sequence — edit a drifted "123"→"456", then bump the quota —
  slot 0 shows "456" and slot 1 stays empty instead of resurrecting "123". And
  Save re-keys to medic#0="456" while deleting the stale medic#1 in one write,
  so there's no orphan and no silent data loss (the regression test asserts both
  the display and the Save).

⏺ Reverted — clean. And the previous fixes are committed (df456d1).

  Root cause (confirmed + reproduced)

  When you swipe-delete a row, _onStageSlotDeletion only marks the slot for
  deletion — a quota reduction. It never touches the slot's note. Then on Save:

  1. Quota drops social 2 → 1 (quotaSets: [social->1] in your log).
  2. The annotation cleanup (_stageSlotAnnotationCleanup →
  computeSlotAnnotationNormalization, keyed off the event's live quota) sees
  social#1 is now out of range with a free gap at social#0, so it drifts the
  note there — re-keys social#1 → social#0 "to avoid losing it." That's your
  updateEventSlotAnnotation {key: social#0}.

  I reproduced it: the "123" note teleports from deleted slot 1 to slot 0.

  The key subtlety: swipe-deleting the row and reducing the quota in the event 
  form are the same operation at the data level — both just set social → 1. So
  the drift isn't specific to the swipe; reducing the quota in the form would
  move the note too. The feature deliberately drifts notes onto a free gap to
  avoid losing them. Your model is the opposite — a note belongs to its slot and
  should die with it. Both are defensible, so this is a product call, not a
  pure bug.

  (Note: whichever we pick, the reduce-then-raise-before-Save preservation still
  holds — that path deletes-then-retracts, it never relied on drift.)

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · When a slot is removed by a quota reduction, what should happen to a note
     that was on it? → Delete it, everywhere (recommended)

⏺ Before I start cutting code — one important realization I owe you, because it
  changes what I committed minutes ago.

  Dropping drift entirely is the right model, but it's bigger than the "two pure
  functions + their tests" I quoted. Here's why: a note only ever appears on a
  different slot than its stored key because of drift. Remove drift and "drifted
  notes" cease to exist — which means:

  - The two fixes I just committed in df456d1 — isContentNoop (no-op on a
  drifted note) and Approach A (suppress a duplicated drifted note on quota
  bump) — become dead code. Their whole premise (a note displayed on a slot ≠
  its stored key) can't occur anymore. staleKey is always null.
  - Their drift regression tests (plus the pre-existing "drifted stored
  annotation" test) now test behavior that no longer exists, so they get
  removed, replaced by no-drift tests + the new "delete the row → note is
  deleted" regression.

  That's all coherent — it's the natural consequence of "notes belong to their
  slot." The end state is simpler (no drift machinery at all). I just don't want
  to silently unwind a commit I made ten minutes ago.

  My plan:
  1. Remove drift from the two reconcile functions → out-of-range notes get
  deleted, never moved.
  2. Update the pure-function unit tests + remove the now-obsolete drift bloc
  tests; add the "delete the noted row → note gone" regression.
  3. Leave isContentNoop / Approach A code in place but dormant (lowest risk),
  and offer to strip that dead machinery as a separate follow-up commit.

  Good to proceed on that basis? (If you'd rather keep drift for the event-form
  path and only special-case swipe-delete, that's the surgical alternative — but
  it's less consistent, which is exactly what you were rejecting.)

※ recap: Fixing gap-annotation bugs on the Shavtzak assignments screen. You just approved dropping "note drift" so deleting a slot deletes its note; I'm waiting for your go-ahead to start that change, which also makes two just-committed fixes dormant. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ Explain again... I don't understand.                                          
  Explain with examples...                                                      

⏺ No problem — let me build it up from scratch with concrete examples. One idea
  explains everything here: drift.

  First: what "drift" means

  Every note is stored in the database under a key tied to a slot index — like
  social#0 (first row), social#1 (second row).

  Normally that matches what you see: social#1 shows on row 1. Simple.

  Drift is when that mapping breaks — a note ends up shown on a different row
  than the slot it's stored under. Here's the only way it happens:

  Start — social quota = 2:
     Row 0:  (empty)
     Row 1:  "123"        ← stored in DB as  social#1

  Now reduce the quota to 1 — only Row 0 survives:
     Row 0:  "123"   ← the app MOVES it here so it isn't "lost"
                       …but it's still stored under social#1

  That mismatch — stored as social#1, but shown on Row 0 — is "drift". The note
  floated to a row that isn't its own. This is a deliberate feature today
  ("don't lose notes"), and it is the source of every bug you've reported.

  All three bugs are the same bug: drift

  Bug 1 — "row marked dirty when I changed nothing" (your first report)
  Drifted "123": stored social#1, shown on Row 0.
  You open it → change nothing → Save.
  The app notices "shown on Row 0 but stored as social#1" and wants to fix the
  key,
  so it marks the row dirty — even though you typed nothing.
  → My Fix 1 (isContentNoop) said: "if the text didn't change, don't mark dirty,
  even if the keys mismatch."

  Bug 2 — "123 reappeared on the new row"
  Drifted "123": stored social#1, shown on Row 0.
  You edit it to "456" (your edit sticks to Row 0).
  You bump quota back to 2 → Row 1 exists again → social#1 ("123") shows on Row
  1 again.
  Result:   Row 0: "456"   Row 1: "123"   ← the note split in two!
  → My Fix 2 (Approach A) said: "hide the original 123 because an edit already
  owns it."

  Bug 3 — "I deleted the row, the note moved" (your current report)
     Row 0:  (empty)
     Row 1:  "123"        ← social#1
  You delete Row 1 → quota drops to 1 → drift moves "123" onto Row 0.
     Row 0:  "123"   ← you wanted it GONE, but it drifted instead.

  See the pattern? Bug 1, Bug 2, and Bug 3 are all just drift misbehaving in 
  different situations.

  What "remove drift" does

  Your rule: a note belongs to its slot and dies with it — it never moves to 
  another row.

     Row 0:  (empty)
     Row 1:  "123"        ← social#1
  Delete Row 1 → quota 1:
     Row 0:  (empty)   ← "123" is DELETED. Nothing drifts anywhere.  ✅ Bug 3
  fixed

  But here's the key consequence — since notes can never be shown on a row other
  than their own:

  - Bug 1 can't happen anymore. A note is always on its own row, so opening it
  and saving unchanged was always a no-op. Fix 1 was only needed for drifted
  notes → nothing left for it to do.
  - Bug 2 can't happen anymore. There's no drifted note to edit, so nothing can
  split/duplicate on a quota bump. Fix 2 → nothing left for it to do.

  So removing drift fixes Bug 3 and dissolves Bugs 1 & 2 at the root. That's why
  I said Fix 1 and Fix 2 become "dead code": the code is still there, but the
  situations it handles (drifted notes) can no longer occur, so it never runs.

  So the choice is

  - Remove drift (what you picked): notes never move; deleting a row deletes its
  note. Fixes Bug 3, and Fixes 1 & 2 become unused leftovers (I'd remove their
  now-meaningless tests, and can clean up the dead code separately).
  - Keep drift, special-case only swipe-delete: notes still drift when you
  reduce quota in the event form, but a swipe-delete deletes the note. Keeps
  Fixes 1 & 2 relevant, but the two paths behave differently — which is the
  inconsistency you were objecting to.

  Want me to go ahead and remove drift?

✻ Baked for 1m 2s

  "social#1") should be also removed from the DB...

⏺ Committed as fd9ffd3. Done — and it does exactly what you asked.

  What changed

  A gap note now belongs to its slot. When a slot disappears — you swipe-delete
  the row, or reduce the quota past it — the note is deleted from the DB
  (FieldValue.delete() on social#1), never moved onto another row.

  Your example:
  Before delete — social quota 2:
     Row 0:  (empty)
     Row 1:  "123"      ← social#1

  Swipe-delete Row 1 → Save:
     Row 0:  (empty)    ← "123" is GONE from the DB. Nothing drifts.  ✅

  Until you press Save, the note shows as a pending-removal row (an off-quota
  "מחוץ למכסה"-style row) so you can see it's going and Save/Discard it — it
  doesn't silently teleport or vanish. On Save, social#1 is removed from the
  database.

  This also cleans up your existing stale social#1 = "123": next time you open
  that event it'll appear as a pending-removal row and get deleted on Save.

  Verification

  - Full suite: 434 tests pass · flutter analyze clean
  - New regression test: "delete the noted row → note removed from the DB, never
  moved to the surviving row"
  - Pure-function unit tests rewritten to assert delete-not-drift; the obsolete
  drift tests removed

  One honest note (as I flagged)

  Since drifted notes can no longer exist, the drift-specific code from the
  previous commit (isContentNoop, Approach A, the staleKey plumbing) is now
  dormant — unreachable, harmless, and its tests are gone. I left it in place
  rather than doing a big risky refactor in the same change. I'd like to strip 
  it in a small follow-up commit so the code doesn't carry misleading drift
  comments. Want me to do that cleanup next?

  To test in the app

  Worktree: /Users/omerbengal/Documents/Github 
  Projects/Shavtzak/.claude/worktrees/empty-slot-gap-annotations → cd shavtzak 
  && flutter run. On /admin/assignments: give a role 2 rows, put a note on the
  2nd, swipe-delete that row, Save → the note is gone and did not move to row 1.
  Same if you reduce the quota via the event form.

  Both commits (df456d1, fd9ffd3) are on feat/empty-slot-gap-annotations, not
  yet pushed/merged.

⏺ Acknowledged — goal is to fix the bug where reducing a role's quota (which
  orphans the 2nd row's label) leaves a staged cleanup that "discard all" can't
  get rid of. Let me gather evidence: the current DB state of the event, plus
  the rebaseline/cleanup code path.

  Called plugin:firebase:firebase, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Confirmed the bug: the DB has roleRequirements.social = 1 but an orphaned 
  social#1 label (labelId set, out of range) still sitting there. The quota
  reduction was committed via event.update, but the orphan-cleanup was only
  staged — and "discard all" threw it away, leaving the label stranded.

  Let me see where RebaselineQuotasForEvent is dispatched and how the event-form
  save flows, to decide where the fix belongs:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ This is the core bug: the event-form quota reduction commits the quota
  (event.update), but the orphan-cleanup is only staged — so "discard all"
  throws it away and strands the social#1 label in the DB. The fix: since the
  quota change is committed, its orphan-cleanup must be committed too (written
  immediately), not staged.

  Let me read the rest of the handler and the cleanup helper to implement this
  cleanly:

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Now I understand the full flow. I'll add a committed-path cleanup that writes
  the orphan-delete immediately (and cleans the cached event so the trailing
  rebuild doesn't re-stage it). Let me add the method right after
  _stageSlotAnnotationCleanup:

           +equirements via                                              
      3645 +  /// event.update, so the annotation cleanup it forces must 
           +be committed too —                                           
      3646 +  /// WRITTEN straight to the DB, never left staged. A staged
           + cleanup here is                                             
      3647 +  /// the asymmetry behind the "reduce a quota, then Discard 
           +All, and the                                                 
      3648 +  /// now-orphaned note is stranded in the DB" bug: the quota
           + drop stays                                                  
      3649 +  /// (committed) while Discard throws away the only staged e
           +ntry that would                                              
      3650 +  /// have removed the orphan. Committing removes the orphan 
           +atomically-enough                                            
      3651 +  /// with the quota change and leaves nothing to strand.    
      3652 +  ///                                                        
      3653 +  /// Slots carrying a live USER edit (auto:false StagedSlotA
           +nnotation) are left                                          
      3654 +  /// untouched — those belong to the user's own staged Save.
      3655 +  Future<void> _commitSlotAnnotationCleanupForEvent(         
      3656 +      Event event, List<Assignment> assignments) async {     
      3657 +    final roleKeys = <String>{};                             
      3658 +    for (final k in event.slotAnnotations.keys) {            
      3659 +      final p = parseSlotAnnotationKey(k);                   
      3660 +      if (p != null) roleKeys.add(p.roleKey);                
      3661 +    }                                                        
      3662 +    if (roleKeys.isEmpty) return;                            
      3663 +                                                             
      3664 +    final ops = <SlotAnnotationWrite>[];                     
      3665 +    for (final roleKey in roleKeys) {                        
      3666 +      final quota = event.roleRequirements[roleKey] ?? 0;    
      3667 +      final filled = assignments                             
      3668 +          .where((a) => a.eventId == event.id && a.roleType =
           += roleKey)                                                   
      3669 +          .map((a) => a.slotIndex);                          
      3670 +      for (final op in computeSlotAnnotationNormalization(   
      3671 +          event.slotAnnotations, roleKey, quota, filled)) {  
      3672 +        final parsed = parseSlotAnnotationKey(op.key);       
      3673 +        if (parsed == null) continue;                        
      3674 +        final slotKey = StagedAssignmentChange.slotKeyFor(   
      3675 +            event.id, parsed.roleKey, parsed.slotIndex);     
      3676 +        if (_stagedSlotAnnotations.containsKey(slotKey)) cont
           +inue; // user edit                                           
      3677 +        ops.add(op);                                         
      3678 +      }                                                      
      3679 +    }                                                        
      3680 +    if (ops.isEmpty) return;                                 
      3681 +                                                             
      3682 +    // Update the cached event FIRST (synchronously, before a
           +ny await) so a                                               
      3683 +    // rebuild that interleaves the writes below sees the alr
           +eady-cleaned map                                             
      3684 +    // and never re-detects the orphan (which would re-stage 
           +it).                                                         
      3685 +    final annotations = Map<String, SlotAnnotation>.from(even
           +t.slotAnnotations);                                          
      3686 +    for (final op in ops) {                                  
      3687 +      if (op.value == null) {                                
      3688 +        annotations.remove(op.key);                          
      3689 +      } else {                                               
      3690 +        annotations[op.key] = op.value!;                     
      3691 +      }                                                      
      3692 +      if (op.staleKey != null) annotations.remove(op.staleKey
           +);                                                           
      3693 +    }                                                        
      3694 +    final cleaned = event.copyWith(slotAnnotations: annotatio
           +ns);                                                         
      3695 +    if (_windowEventsMap.containsKey(event.id)) {            
      3696 +      _windowEventsMap[event.id] = cleaned;                  
      3697 +    } else if (_extraPastEventsMap.containsKey(event.id)) {  
      3698 +      _extraPastEventsMap[event.id] = cleaned;               
      3699 +    }                                                        
      3700 +                                                             
      3701 +    // Commit each cleanup write. A failure leaves the DB orp
           +han in place; the                                            
      3702 +    // next quota change or reload re-detects and retries — n
           +o staged residue.                                            
      3703 +    for (final op in ops) {                                  
      3704 +      try {                                                  
      3705 +        await _eventRepository.updateSlotAnnotation(         
      3706 +            event.id, op.key, op.value,                      
      3707 +            staleKey: op.staleKey);                          
      3708 +      } catch (_) {                                          
      3709 +        // best-effort; converges on the next cleanup pass   
      3710 +      }                                                      
      3711 +    }                                                        
      3712 +  }                                                          
      3713 +                                                             
      3714    /// Rebuild slots using pre-loaded data (for real-time upda
            tes)
      3715    Future<void> _onRebuildAssignmentSlotsFromData(
      3716      RebuildAssignmentSlotsFromData rebuildEvent,

  Made 1 scratchpad edit +31, committed b1eea01 (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Fixed and committed as b1eea01.

  Root cause

  An event-form quota reduction is committed immediately by event.update, but
  the orphan-annotation cleanup it forces was only staged. That split is the
  bug: pressing Discard All threw away the staged cleanup while the committed
  quota drop stayed — so the now-orphaned social#1 label was stranded in the DB
  forever (I confirmed it's still there: roleRequirements.social = 1 but
  slotAnnotations.social#1 present).

  The fix

  _onRebaselineQuotasForEvent now writes the cleanup immediately (new
  _commitSlotAnnotationCleanupForEvent) instead of staging it:
  - Deletes each out-of-range note straight from the DB, and cleans the cached
  event first so the trailing rebuild can't re-detect/re-stage it.
  - Skips any slot with a live user edit (those stay staged for the user's
  Save).
  - Result: nothing is left staged → nothing for Discard to strand. Matches your
  model — reducing the quota deletes the note.

  The swipe-delete path is unchanged (its quota change is staged, so its cleanup
  rightly stays staged and Saves/Discards together with it).

  One behavior change to flag: reduce-then-raise via the event form no longer
  preserves the note — it's deleted on the reduce (committed), consistent with
  the "a note belongs to its slot" model you chose.

  Verification

  - Full suite: 435 tests pass · flutter analyze clean
  - Reworked the RebaselineQuotasForEvent tests (stage → commit) + a new REPRO:
  "reduce, then Discard All → nothing stranded, label already gone from the DB."

  Two notes

  1. The existing social#1 orphan on event be47e00c is leftover data from the
  old bug. It's loaded via the stream path (which still stages), so on next load
  it'll show as a pending-removal row / "1 unsaved change" that one Save
  clears. Want me to just delete it from the DB directly so your test event is
  clean?
  2. This is commit b1eea01 on feat/empty-slot-gap-annotations (worktree
  /Users/omerbengal/Documents/Github 
  Projects/Shavtzak/.claude/worktrees/empty-slot-gap-annotations). Four commits
  now stack on this branch; none pushed/merged.

  To test: in that worktree, cd shavtzak && flutter run → give a role 2 rows,
  put a label on the 2nd, reduce the quota via the event form, then press
  Discard All → the label is gone from the DB and there's nothing stranded.

⏺ You're right to be frustrated — I've been fixing the reported case each time
  without testing the sibling cases around it. Let me stop guessing and actually
  reproduce this, then test the whole matrix instead of one case.

  From your screenshots the situation is: two empty entryScreening rows — row 0
  = "123", row 1 = "456" — and you deleted the row you meant to delete but the
  wrong note survived. Let me look at how a row-delete maps notes to slots, and
  reproduce it:

  Read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Let me reproduce the mechanism precisely with the pure normalization function
  rather than guess — this is deterministic:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Confirmed deterministically: with notes [#0="123", #1="456"], reducing the
  quota to 1 produces delete medic#1 — it always deletes the highest-index note
  ("456") and keeps the lower one ("123"). It never shifts the lower notes up.

  Root cause

  Gap notes are keyed by absolute slot index (entryScreening#0, #1). Removing a
  row just lowers the quota, and the cleanup deletes whatever note is now out of
  range — the highest one. It has no idea which row you removed, and it doesn't
  renumber. So:
  - Remove the last row → fine (its note is the highest → removed).
  - Remove a non-last row (e.g. row 0 = "123") → wrong: it deletes the last note
  ("456") and leaves "123" — exactly what you saw.

  The correct behavior: deleting row i should delete row i's note and shift the
  notes below it up (like deleting a list item), so removing "123" leaves "456"
  on the one remaining row.

  Before I code it — one thing, so I fix the exact path and not a sibling

  You're right that I keep patching one case at a time, so let me pin the
  trigger and then cover the whole matrix. Which did you do here?

      114    });

      117 +                                                              
      118 +/// After TARGETED row-deletions remove [deletedIndices] from 
          +a role that had                                               
      119 +/// [oldQuota] slots, repack the surviving notes contiguous fr
          +om 0 — the note                                               
      120 +/// parallel of `_reindexRoleAfterDeletion` (which repacks ass
          +ignments). A                                                  
      121 +/// deleted row's note is dropped; a note on a row BELOW a del
          +eted row shifts up                                            
      122 +/// to follow its row. Returns the writes (rewrite / delete) t
          +hat turn the stored                                           
      123 +/// notes into that layout.                                   
      124 +///                                                           
      125 +/// This is what makes "swipe-delete the 2nd of two rows, keep
          + the 1st" work.                                               
      126 +/// Contrast [computeSlotAnnotationNormalization], which only 
          +deletes notes that                                            
      127 +/// fell out of range (correct for "remove the LAST row" — an 
          +event-form                                                    
      128 +/// decrement — but it deletes the highest note regardless of 
          +WHICH row you                                                 
      129 +/// removed, and never shifts).                               
      130 +List<SlotAnnotationWrite> computeNoteReindexAfterDeletion(    
      131 +  Map<String, SlotAnnotation> eventSlotAnnotations,           
      132 +  String roleKey,                                             
      133 +  int oldQuota,                                               
      134 +  Set<int> deletedIndices,                                    
      135 +) {                                                           
      136 +  if (deletedIndices.isEmpty || oldQuota <= 0) return const []
          +;                                                             
      137 +                                                              
      138 +  final byIndex = <int, SlotAnnotation>{};                    
      139 +  eventSlotAnnotations.forEach((k, v) {                       
      140 +    final p = parseSlotAnnotationKey(k);                      
      141 +    if (p != null && p.roleKey == roleKey && !v.isEmpty) {    
      142 +      byIndex[p.slotIndex] = v;                               
      143 +    }                                                         
      144 +  });                                                         
      145 +                                                              
      146 +  // Old indices [0, oldQuota) that survive the deletion, in a
          +scending order —                                              
      147 +  // they repack onto new indices 0,1,2,… so surviving[newIdx]
          + is the OLD index                                             
      148 +  // whose note now belongs at newIdx.                        
      149 +  final surviving = [                                         
      150 +    for (var i = 0; i < oldQuota; i++)                        
      151 +      if (!deletedIndices.contains(i)) i,                     
      152 +  ];                                                          
      153 +                                                              
      154 +  final writes = <SlotAnnotationWrite>[];                     
      155 +  // Touch every old in-range index: indices < surviving.lengt
          +h take the                                                    
      156 +  // repacked note; the rest (freed by the deletion) are clear
          +ed.                                                           
      157 +  for (var k = 0; k < oldQuota; k++) {                        
      158 +    final oldNote = byIndex[k];                               
      159 +    final newNote = k < surviving.length ? byIndex[surviving[k
          +]] : null;                                                    
      160 +    if (oldNote == newNote) continue; // unchanged at this ind
          +ex                                                            
      161 +    writes.add((key: slotAnnotationKey(roleKey, k), value: new
          +Note, staleKey: null));                                       
      162 +  }                                                           
      163 +  return writes;                                              
      164 +}

⏺ 447 pass. Committing:

  Made 1 scratchpad edit +30, committed 4855219 (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Fixed and committed as 4855219. And I tested the matrix this time, not just
  your one case.

  Root cause

  Gap notes are keyed by absolute slot index (entryScreening#0, #1). A
  row-delete only lowers the quota, and the cleanup deletes whatever note is now
  out of range — the highest one — regardless of which row you removed, and it
  never shifts the lower notes up. Assignments were already repacked on delete
  (_reindexRoleAfterDeletion); notes were the missing half.

  The fix

  computeNoteReindexAfterDeletion — the note parallel of the assignment reindex.
  Given the pre-delete quota and the exact deleted indices, it repacks the
  surviving notes contiguous from 0: the deleted row's note is dropped, and rows
  below it shift up. Wired into the save right next to the assignment reindex,
  and it defers to any already-staged annotation so a delete is never
  double-written.

  So on your event: swipe-delete the "123" row → "456" shifts up and survives.

  What I tested (the matrix)

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │                    Case                    │        Result        │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ [123, 456] delete row 0                    │ keeps 456 ✅         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ [123, 456] delete row 1 (last)             │ keeps 123 ✅         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ [A, B, C] delete middle (B)                │ [A, C] ✅            │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ [A, B, C] delete first                     │ [B, C] ✅            │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ delete two rows at once                    │ survivors compact ✅ │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ delete an empty row above a noted row      │ note shifts up ✅    │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ delete a noted row whose survivor is empty │ note dropped ✅      │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  10 pure-function cases + 3 full StageSlotDeletion → Save integration cases.
  Full suite 447 pass, analyze clean.

  Boundaries I deliberately left as-is (so you know)

  - Event-form decrement (not swipe) still removes the last row's note — that's
  the standard "reduce the count" behavior, and it's what you'd expect there. To
  drop a specific row's note, swipe that row (now fixed).
  - Filled + empty rows mixed: the note reindex uses the same surviving-index
  ordering as the assignment reindex, so a filled row's assignment and its note
  stay in lockstep. Not separately integration-tested — say the word and I'll
  add that case too.

⏺ Good question — let me check the mixed case carefully, because
  _reindexRoleAfterDeletion packs assignments contiguous to the front (empty
  slots go to the back), and my note reindex currently uses a plain list-shift.
  Those two can diverge. Let me reason it out with a concrete case:

  [memberA@0, empty+"X"@1, memberB@2], delete memberA@0:
  - Assignment reindex → memberB packs to slot 0, empty slot at 1.
  - My current note reindex → "X" shifts to slot 0… which is now memberB's slot.
  Collision — "X" becomes a stray dormant note on a filled slot. That's a real
  bug.

  So the note reindex must be filled-aware (empty notes land after the packed
  assignments). Let me fix the function and add the mixed coverage:

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

  Made 1 scratchpad edit +23, committed f2ae265 (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes — adding those tests exposed a real bug, now fixed and committed as
  f2ae265.

  The issue the mixed case revealed

  _reindexRoleAfterDeletion packs surviving assignments contiguous to the front,
  leaving empty slots at the back. My note reindex used a plain shift-up, which
  diverges:

  [member@0, empty+"X"@1, member@2]   delete member@0

  assignment reindex →  member(was@2) packs to slot 0,  empty at slot 1
  plain note shift-up →  "X" moves to slot 0  ← collides onto the assignment!

  So "X" would have landed on the filled slot as a stray dormant note (invisible
  until that member is later removed, then it'd resurface). A latent data bug.

  The fix

  computeNoteReindexAfterDeletion now takes the role's filledIndices and packs
  the same way the assignment reindex does — surviving filled slots to 0..k-1
  (each keeping its own dormant note), surviving empty slots and their gap notes
  to k..newQuota-1. With no assignments (the pure-empty case you reported) it's
  identical to before, so that behavior is unchanged.

  Tests added for mixed rows

  - Pure function (3): delete a filled row → empty note packs to the right slot;
  [m@0, empty+X@1, m@2] delete m@0 → X stays at slot 1, no collision; dormant
  notes follow their assignment (Q moves with member@2 to slot 0).
  - Integration (1): delete a filled first row of [member@0, empty+"X"@1] → "X"
  shifts to slot 0 through the real StageSlotDeletion → Save flow (verifies
  filledIndices is wired from getCurrentAssignments).

  Full suite 451 pass, analyze clean. Six commits now on
  feat/empty-slot-gap-annotations (worktree /Users/omerbengal/Documents/Github 
  Projects/Shavtzak/.claude/worktrees/empty-slot-gap-annotations), none pushed.

  One boundary I'll name so it's explicit: this makes notes consistent with how
  the app already re-packs assignments (filled-to-front). If you'd actually
  prefer visual-order-preserving deletion for both notes and assignments (so a
  filled row doesn't jump ahead of an empty one), that's a deliberate change to
  _reindexRoleAfterDeletion too — say the word and we can treat it as its own
  task.

✻ Baked for 8m 43s

❯ Explain the "One boundary" and the "visual-order-preserving deletion"         

⏺ Sure. This is about what happens to the other rows when you delete one,
  specifically when a role has a mix of assigned (filled) and empty (note-only)
  rows.

  The setup

  Say a role has 3 rows (quota 3):

  Row 0:  משה          ← assigned (filled)
  Row 1:  (empty)  · note "צריך רכב"   ← empty, has a gap note
  Row 2:  דוד          ← assigned (filled)

  You swipe-delete Row 0 (משה). Now there are 2 rows left. The question is:
  which layout do you get?

  The two models

  Model A — "filled-to-front" (what the app does today)
  Surviving assignments pack up to the top; empty slots drop to the bottom:

  Row 0:  דוד                      ← דוד jumped up past the note-row
  Row 1:  (empty) · "צריך רכב"

  Model B — "visual-order-preserving" (spreadsheet/list style)
  Delete the row, everything below slides up by exactly one, keeping the same
  order:

  Row 0:  (empty) · "צריך רכב"      ← the note-row stays above דוד
  Row 1:  דוד

  The difference: in A, deleting a row can reorder the survivors (an assignment
  leapfrogs an empty row above it). In B, the survivors keep their exact
  relative order.

  Why I called it a "boundary"

  The app has always used Model A for assignments — that's the existing
  _reindexRoleAfterDeletion, not something I introduced. My fix made the notes 
  follow the same model so notes and assignments agree. That's the safe choice:
  if I'd made notes use Model B while assignments stayed on Model A, they'd
  disagree — which is exactly the collision bug I just fixed (a note landing on
  a filled row).

  So the "boundary" is: I aligned notes to the app's existing deletion behavior,
  rather than changing that behavior. Switching to Model B would mean changing
  _reindexRoleAfterDeletion too (the assignment re-indexing) so both notes and
  assignments preserve visual order — a bigger, separate task that touches core
  assignment handling, not just the gap-notes feature.

  Two things worth knowing

  1. For your actual reported bug, this is moot. When all rows are empty (no
  assignments to "pack to front"), Model A and Model B are identical — both just
  slide the note-rows up in order. The distinction only appears when filled and
  empty rows are interleaved.
  2. Whether Model A is even a problem is a judgment call. It's only visible if
  you delete a row that sits above a filled row with an empty note-row in
  between, and the effect is "the assigned person jumps up a slot." Since a
  role's rows are essentially a pool of slots, most people won't notice — but it
  is technically a reordering.

  So the choice is: keep Model A (done — notes and assignments consistent, all
  tests green) or move both to Model B (visual-order-preserving deletion, a
  deliberate change to the assignment re-index). Happy to do the latter as its
  own task if that's the behavior you want — just let me know.

⏺ Key finding from the tests: the reindex tests all use fully-filled roles (no
  empty slot between the deletion and a survivor), so pack and shift produce the
  same result there — Model B won't break them. And Model B is actually
  cleaner: everything (assignments, notes, staged adds) shifts by "deleted rows
  below it," so notes and assignments move together and can never collide.

⏺ 450 pass. Committing Model B:

  Made 1 scratchpad edit +29, committed 44d4883 (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Done — Model B is implemented and committed as 44d4883.

  What changed

  Deleting a row no longer reorders the survivors. Every surviving row — filled
  or empty — now shifts up by the number of deleted rows below it, keeping its
  position:

  [member@0, empty+"X"@1, member@2]   delete member@0

  before (pack):  [member@0, empty+"X"@1]   ← member jumped ahead of the note
  now  (shift):   [empty+"X"@0, member@1]   ← order preserved

  How

  The key realization: once assignments shift (instead of packing to the front),
  notes and assignments use the identical mapping — newIndex = oldIndex − 
  deletedRowsBelow — so they move in lockstep and can never collide. That let
  me:
  - _reindexRoleAfterDeletion: pack → shift.
  - computeNoteReindexAfterDeletion: drop the filled-awareness from the previous
  commit, back to a plain shift.
  - Both driven by the same deleted-indices set, computed once.

  Why nothing broke

  When a role has no empty slots, shift == the old contiguous pack — so all 
  existing reindex/manual-add tests (which are filled-only) pass unchanged. Full
  suite 450 pass, analyze clean.

  Tests

  - Note pure-function suite trimmed to the plain-shift cases (the
  filled-awareness it used to need is gone).
  - New save_test: a survivor with an empty slot between it and the deletion
  shifts 2 → 1 instead of jumping to slot 0 — the exact behavior that diverges
  from the old model.

  One honest boundary

  There's one edge I did not separately test: delete + a same-role manual-add + 
  an empty slot in between. For the common case (no empty slot between), the
  manual-add still fills the freed slot exactly as before — the existing
  manual-add tests confirm it. But with an interleaved empty slot, a manual-add
  now appends (shifts to the end) rather than filling the empty gap — a natural
  consequence of "preserve order," but untested. Say the word if you want me to
  pin that case down too.

  Seven commits now on feat/empty-slot-gap-annotations (worktree
  /Users/omerbengal/Documents/Github 
  Projects/Shavtzak/.claude/worktrees/empty-slot-gap-annotations), none pushed.

✻ Baked for 16m 10s

❯ Explain the edge case with example                                            

⏺ Sure. This edge case needs three things to happen in the same unsaved batch: a
  row deletion, a manual-add of a new member, and an empty slot sitting between
  them. Without the manual-add it's just clean order-preservation; without the
  empty slot, Model B and Model A are identical.

  The example

  A role with quota 3:

  Row 0:  משה          ← assigned
  Row 1:  (empty)      ← unfilled slot
  Row 2:  דוד          ← assigned

  In one batch, the admin does two things:
  1. Swipe-deletes Row 0 (משה).
  2. Manual-adds a new member, רון — which appends at the bottom (staged at
  index 3).

  So the staged picture is:

  Row 0:  משה   (marked for deletion)
  Row 1:  (empty)
  Row 2:  דוד
  Row 3:  רון   (new manual-add)

  Now Save. Everything with deleted-rows-below = 1 shifts up by one.

  Model A (old — pack assignments to front):
  Row 0:  דוד       ← assignments packed to the top
  Row 1:  רון        ← the new member fills a top slot
  Row 2:  (empty)   ← empty pushed to the bottom

  Model B (new — shift, preserve order):
  Row 0:  (empty)   ← empty stays where it was, shifted up
  Row 1:  דוד
  Row 2:  רון        ← the new member is appended at the bottom

  The two visible differences

  1. Where the empty slot ends up. Model A pushes it to the bottom; Model B
  keeps its relative position, so here it lands at the top (Row 0) with filled
  rows below it.
  2. Where the new member lands. Model A has רון fill the freed slot near the
  top; Model B appends רון at the end and leaves the empty gap alone.

  Is it a problem?

  Not a data bug — both layouts are fully in-quota (quota = 3 − 1 delete + 1 add
  = 3), nothing is stranded, and there are no collisions (I verified: survivors
  and the manual-add can't land on the same index). It's purely a layout 
  choice:

  - Model B is internally consistent ("delete the top, everything slides up, new
  adds go to the bottom").
  - But an empty slot sitting above filled rows can look odd — most people
  expect empty slots at the bottom.

  That's the tension: strict order-preservation (Model B) vs. "keep empties at
  the bottom / let a new add fill the gap" (a bit of Model A). You can't fully
  have both — pushing empties down is a reorder.

  It's an uncommon combination (delete and add and an interleaved empty, all
  before one Save), which is why I left it untested and flagged it rather than
  guessing. If you want, I can pin the desired behavior for it — e.g. "after a
  delete+add, still shift for order, but sink trailing empty slots to the
  bottom" — and add tests. Just tell me which layout you'd want for that case.

✻ Brewed for 1m 11s

※ recap: Goal: fix gap-annotation row-deletion bugs on the assignments screen. All done and committed on feat/empty-slot-gap-annotations (Model B, 450 tests pass). Next: decide whether the delete-plus-add empty-slot edge case needs handling, or wrap up.

❯ the situation in model B looks great!                                         
  SO what is the problem?                                                       

⏺ You're right — there isn't one. I over-flagged it.

  Let me be straight: what I labeled an "edge case" wasn't a bug or a broken
  behavior. The Model B layout you saw is correct and internally consistent —
  everything shifts up, the new member appends at the end, and (I verified the
  math) it's always in-quota with no collisions and nothing stranded. My
  aesthetic worry — "an empty slot above filled rows looks odd" — is just a
  preference, and you've said it looks right. So there's nothing to fix.

  The one legitimate thing behind my flag was narrower and boring: that specific
  three-way combination (delete + manual-add + an interleaved empty slot) isn't
  covered by a test. So right now it's "described and reasoned-through" but not
  proven — which means a future change could silently alter it and nothing
  would catch it.

  There's also a historical reason I was cautious: the old pack-to-front code
  existed partly to stop a manual-add from being stranded off-quota. I checked
  that Model B doesn't reintroduce that (the add shifts down by the same number
  of deletions that the quota drops by, so it stays in-quota) — but "I checked
  by hand" is weaker than "there's a test."

  So the honest answer: no problem with the behavior — just a coverage gap. Want
  me to add a test that locks in exactly the layout you liked ([empty, דוד, 
  רון] after delete-top + add)? That turns "reasoned-through" into "protected,"
  and costs nothing behavior-wise.
